MINISTRY OF PERSONNET,, PUBUC GRIEVANCES * 
PENSIONS 

(Department of Personnel and Training) 

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 8th July, 1992 

S.O. 1947.—in exercise of the powers conferred bv sub¬ 
section (1) of Section 5 read with section 6 of the Delhi 
Special Police Establishment Act, 1946 (Act No, 25 of 1946), 
the Central Government with the consent of the State Gov¬ 
ernment of Andhia Pradesh accorded vide Home (SC-A) 
Department GORt. No. 2308 dated 7th July, 1992, hereby 
extends the powers and jurisdiction of the members of the 
Delhi Special Police Establishment to the whole of the State 
of Andhra Pradesh for further investigation of the offences 
punishable under Sections 147, 148. 324, 326, 307, 302 r/w 
120-B r/w 109 of the Indian Penal Code and Explosive 
Substances Act, 1908 arising out of Crime No. 334/88 of 
Vijayawada Law and Order III, Town Police Station in res¬ 
pect of the incident that occurred on 26th December, 1988 
at early hours Bandar Road, Vijayawada in which S/Shri 
V. M, Ranga Rao, MLA was killed, in which case charge 
sheet has already been filed by the State CID of the Gov¬ 
ernment of Andhra Pradesh in tho court of learned Judicial 
First Class Magistrate, West & South, Saroornagar vide PRC 
No. 4'89 against Shri Devineni Rajasekhar, MLA and 
others. 

[No. 228/37/92-AVD-II] 
A, K. PARASHAR, Dy. Secy.

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 15th July, 1992 

S.O. 1955.—Whereas tne Joint Secretary to iho Govern¬ 
ment of India specially empowered under sub-section (1) 
of Section 3 of the Conservation of Foreign Exchange and 
Prevention of Smuggling Activties Act, 1974 (52 of 19741 
issued under F, No. 673/356/91-Cus. VIII dated 19-8-91 
under the said subjection directing that Shri Devkinandan 
(S, Raju, Sjo. Late Shri Banwari Lai, A-6|8, Rana Praiap 
Bagh, Delhi be detained and kept in custody in the Central 
Jail, Tihar, New Delhi with a view to preventing him from 
acting in any rnunner prejudicial to the augmentation of 
foreign exchange and from dealing in smuggled goods other¬ 
wise than by engaging in transporting or concealing or keep 
ing smuggled goods. 

2. Whereas the Centra] Goverment has reasons to beheve 
that the afoicsaid person has absconded or is concealing 
himself so that the order cannot be executed: 

3. Now, therfore, in exercise of power conferred by 
clause (b) of sub-section (1) of Section 7 of the said Act, 
the Central Government hereby directs the aforesaid person 
to appear before the Commissioner of Police, New Delhi 
within 7 days of the publieaiton of this Oder in the official 
gazette.

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 15th July, 1992 

S.O. 1956,—Whereas the Joint Sccretaiy to the Government 
of India, specially empowered under sub-section (1) of sec¬ 
tion 3 of the Conservation of Foreign Exchange and preven¬ 
tion of Smuggling Activities Act, 1974 (52 of 1974J issued 
under F. No, 673|378|91-CTJS. VIII dated 5-9-91 under the 
said sub-section directing that Shri Paras Patanlal Jain 
©Punamiya @Rajeshdas Sharma @Sanjav Sharma Flat 
No, 6, II floor, Arihant Apartment, Station Road, Bhayander- 
(W), Thane (Distt.), Maharashtra, be detained and kept in 
custody in the Central Prison, Bombay with a view to preven- 
ing him from acting in any manner prejudicial to the con¬ 
servation of foreign exchange. 

2. Whereas the Central Government has reasons to believe 
that the aforesaid person has absconded or is concealing 
himself so that the order cannot be executed; 

3. Now, therefore, in exercise of power conferred by 
clause (b) of sub-section (1) of section 7 of the said Act, the 
Central Government hereby directs the aforesaid person to 
appear before the Director General of Police, Maharashtra. 
Bombay within 7 days of the publication of this order in the 
official Gazette.

MINISTRY OF COAL 
New Delhi, the 10th July, 1992 

S.O. 1964.—Wher as by the notification of the Government of India in the Ministry of 
Coal No. S.O. 2325 dated the 29th August, 1991, published in the Gazette of India, Pait II, 
Section 3, sub-section (ii), at pages 3576 to 3582, issued under sub-section (1) of Section 7 of 
the Coal Bearing Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 1957 (20 of 1957), the Central Govern¬ 
ment gave notice of its intention to acquire the lands described in the Schedule appended to 
that notification; 

And whereas it has been brought to the notice of the Central Government that certain errors 
of printing nature have occurred in the publication of the said notification in the Gazette; 


Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 7 of the 
said Act and of the other powers enabling it in this behaif, the Central Government hereby amends 
the schedule appended to the said notification as follows :

CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL 
AT CALCUTTA 

Reference No. 107 of 1985s 

PARTIES ; 

Employers in relation to the management of Bhanora 
Colliery of M|s. H. C. Ltd.

On behalf of Management --Mr. R. S. Murthy, Advo¬ 
cate. 

On behalf of Workmen—None. 

STATE : West Bengal. INDUSTRY : Coal. 

AWARD 

One Bineswari Charnar, who died in harness on August 
27, 1935 at the Central Hospital, Kalla, has been claimed to 
be working as a Wagon Loader at Bhanora Colliery of 
Messrs Eastern Coalfields Limited, Since 1972. 

2. It has been stated that she was a “workman” and was 
covered under the Coal Wage Board’s Recommendations and 
her conditions of service and terms of employment were 
governed by the National Coal Wage Agreement-Ill, which, 
in Clause 9.4.2 postulates some beneficial service conditions 
for workmen dying in harness and in terms thereof, it has 
been stated that the son of the deceased, perhaps Sri Surender 
Chamar, filed an application for necessary benefits on Jan¬ 
uary 27, 1986 to the employer. This application litis not 
been produced in this proceedings. 

3. It was also alleged that there was denial by the em¬ 
ployers in giving the necessary benefits to the dependant 
son in terms of clause 9.4.2 as aforesaid and a $ such, the 
present dispute, regarding the validity or otherwise o! the 
action of the management has been referred for adjudica¬ 
tion before this Tribunal by the appropriate government by 
Order No. L-19012(1.58)/86-D. 1V(B) dated 10-8-1987. 

4. On such facts, it has been claimed that the said tie- 
pendant son of the deceased, was entitled to the benefits 
of the sa'd Clause 9.4.2, which has been wrongfully, irre¬ 
gularly and illegally refused to him. 

5. In their written statement, the employers have taken 
preliminary objections regarding the maintainability of the 
reference, as according to them, the employee, who died in 
harness, was a casual labour and as such, either she nor 
any of her heirs could claim the benefits under section 2(K) 
of the Industrial Disputes Act 3947, as the said employee 
could not be considered as a workman under the Ac P and 
furthermore. When there was no relationship of employer , 
and employee between the said claimant and the manage¬ 
ment. 

6 . It was further indented that even under clause 9.4.2 
as aforesaid, the claimant could not legally maintain his 
claim since he has not been able to prove his case of heir¬ 
ship legally or otherwise. It was also indicated that such 
casual Wagon Loaders, as the deceased employee was 
employed for meeting the tequirement of the management 
for service of Wagon Loaders over and above regular and 
permanent Wagon Loaders and conditions under which they 
are appointed as such, have been mentioned in paragraph 
13 of Chapter-XV at page 154 of Volume-1 of Central Wage 
Board for* Coal Mining Industry. Such recommendations 
were indicated by Mr. Murthy, to be a non-statutc-ry one 
and they have no legal effect since the Central Government 
has not accepted them. 

7. There was a rejoinder filed by ana on behalf of the 
employee concerned out strangely enough, the material state¬ 
ments as contained in paragraph 6 of the employers Vm'ten 
statement have not been categorically and appropriately 
denied. 


8. From the order sheet, it will appear that initially 
there were appearance entered by and on behalf of the 
Union, representing the claimant and some documents 
through an application dated January 9, 1989 were filed. 
Thereafter, the employers have filed their available docu¬ 
ments and even inspire of due service, the concerned Union 
or representatives have not taken appropriate steps to 
represent the said Union or the case of the applicant. In 
fact, at the time of hearing of the proceedings today also, 
inspite of due receipt of the notice, nofady appeared lor 
the Union or the said workman. 

9. The submissions of Mr. Murthy that there was no 
industrial dispute or the applicant concerned, could not 
claimed to be involved in ♦bis reference, a? there was no 
community of interest with the other employees, 1 think, on 
consideration of the definition of Industrial Dispute and 
workman, have no substance. 

10. It is true that Clause (i) of paragraph 9.4.2 as men¬ 
tioned earlier, has act mentioned about permanent or casual 
employees. But, even then, tbs submissions of Mr. Murthy 
that since the employee dying in hsrness was' a casual work¬ 
men and could not claim any permanency ns a matter of 
right, so her heir, on her death, cannot also reasonably 
claim for any permanent employment. A part from the 
above, it should also be noted that the fact that the apyli- 
claini for any permanent employment. A part from*\he 
legally or beyond any reasonable doubt, as neither the Union 
nor the employee has tendered any evidence and proved the 
application dated January 27, 1986, its mentioned earlier. 

11. Mr. Murthy of course, in support of his submissions 
referred to several celebrated decisions as merit oned in 
paragraphs 2(b) and 3(a) of the written Statement of the 
employers but I think, since the initial onus to establish the 
character, of the applicant as heir of the deceased employee 
itas not been established, so i am. not required to consi ler 
these judgements or the applicability of them hi this. case. 
L should further be noted that the employers also, in view 
of the fact that no evidence Was tendered' by the applicant, 
ejected not .to tender any oral evidence, but they only pro¬ 
duced certain records, xerox copies where of, ate kept with 
the file. 

12. For the views as expressed, J think the Reference can¬ 
not bo answered in the affirmative and jit 1 favour of the 
workman. 

This is my Award. 

Calcutta, the 9ih June, 1992. 

MANASH NATH ROY, Presiding Officer

IN THE COURT OF THE INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL, 
KOLLAM 

(Dated, this the 3rd day of June, 1992) 
PRESENT : 

Sri. C. N. Sasidhuan, Industrial Tribunal 
IN 

Industrial Dispute No. 5|S7 

BETWEEN 

The Director, Government of India Production Centres, 
Ettumanoor, Kottayam Distr ct, Kerala. 

(By Sri A. A. Abdul Hassan, Adi. Central Government 
Standing Counsel, Ernakulam). 

AND 

The General Secretary, Central Government Small Scale 
Industries Grganisa'ion Employees Union, Ettuma¬ 
noor, Kottayam Dis'rict. 

(By Sri R. Rajasekharan Pillai, Advocate, Ernakulam) 
SUPPLEMENTARY AWARD 

As per Order No. L-42011/81-D.II(B) dated 5-3-1987 the 
Government of India have referied this Industrial dispute 
for adjudication to this Tribunal. 

2. Altogether five issues have been refeired for adjudica¬ 
tion. After considering the con'entions advanced by both 
sides and evidence adduced befoie tbi' Tribunal, by award 
dated 12 12.1988 this Tribunal allowed issue numbers 2 to 4 
in favour of the workmen and nega’ived the claim under 
ssue No. 1. Both sides challenged the award passed by this 
Tribunal before Central Administrative Tribunal Ernakulam 
(CAT for short) as per OA No. 403/89 and OA No. 94/90. 
The CAT as per decision dated 31.8 1990 quashed the award 
of this Tribunal so far as it relates to issue No. I and a 
portion of issue No. Ill with regard to claim of uniform 
and directed ths Tribunal to consider these claims a fresh. 
That is how this matter is How before this Tribunal. 

3. I shall summarise below issues numbers I and III. 

I. “Whether 7 the Director of the Government of India 
Production Centres, Ettumanooi was justified in 
fixing 36 Helper Mistrj.es recruited n 1971- 7 2 as 
mistr'es at minimum of the scales, 110-155 af'er 
one year of their service n the lower scale, or 
whether they should hate been given a higher start 
in the scale as ner th ;ir appointment orders? If so, 
what should be 7” 

HI. “Whether the demand of Helpers under the Director 
Government of India Production Cen're, Ettuma¬ 
noor for quasi-permanency, confirmation, provision 
of uniforms, gratuity, pension and other service 
benefits applicable to regular employees is legal 
and justified ? If so, to what extent and subject to 
what conditions.” 

Issue No. t. The case of the uni on is that 36 permanent 
posts of mistries in the scale of Rs. 110-155 was Iving vacant 
at Government of India Production Centre, Ettumanoor 
(Production Centre for Short) which was created by Gov¬ 
ernment of India. The Director of Production Centre (the 
Director for short) requested the Employment Exchange 
specifying the post, qualification etc. and the exchange spon¬ 
sored various candidates and 35 persons were selected as 
mistries (Class-M), Group C posh But offered a lower post 
of helper mistry (Class-lVl Group D in the scale of 
Rs. 75-95. Further case is that there was an undertaking to 
give a higher start in ths scale of Rs. 110-155 after seeing 
their performance for a few months. According to the union 
the appointment orders issued arc not in accordance with 
the instructions of the Government of India These persons 
continued for one year ard they wre appointed in the scale 
of 110-155. The case of 'he union is that the D rector is 
not competent to create a post, l.-desicnation and de-grading 
of these workers as helper mistry (Class-fV) is unjust and 


illegal since no such power has been delegated to the Direc¬ 
tor. Further contention is that the Director violated employ¬ 
ment Exchange Act, 1959, violated instruct ons regarding 
alteration, and conditions of recruitment and service, violat¬ 
ed Government of India, Ministry of Finance circular dated 
1-6-1962, not obtained prior concurrence of Ministry of 
Finance for lowering the qualification etc. According to the 
management the Director in exercise of the powers vested m 
h'm as the appointing authority rightly appointed this helper 
mistries and under GFR 77 tne appointing authority may 
make excess post in a lower scale of pay to a post carrying 
higher scale of pay. GFR 77 reads thus : 

“In the case of non-gazetted es'ablishments divided 
,'nto separate units or cadres carrying different 
scales of pay, an authority competent to make 
appointments in tnat establishment on both units 
or cadres may make excess appoin'ments in a 
lower unit of sadre against equal or greater num¬ 
ber of vacancies left unfilled in the higher unit or 
cadre." 

4. The post of mistries and helpers ire two un’ts of the 
cadre and Director of Prodtic ion Centre was the appointing 
authority. It is no* disputed that the appointment was in a 
non-gazetted establishment. If is also not disputed that as 
per memorandum No. 75 SSI(cH 13)|62 dated 18-9-i962 in 
Ext. M10 Award of Industrial Tribunal Madras and M8 
judgment of the H gh Court of Kerala and ir. the argument 
notes filed this Tribunal on behalf of management, the Presi¬ 
dent of India delegated the powers to make appointments to 
Class TV post and Class III p->st where the scale of pay does 
not exceed Rs. 300|- PM The Director of Pioduction Centre 
a« empowered as stated above offered appointment to the 
candida'es who were called for interview as is evident from 
Ext. W2. The scale of nay of Rs. 75-95 is clearly stated. 
It was also offered to g/ve a higher scale of Rs 110-155 after 
seeing the performance of few months. Ext W2 also refers 
the terms and condtic’s of appointment. This offer Wes 
accepted bv all the workers wlio are now before this 
Tribunal. Ext Ml is one of the undertaking of acctp ance 
submitted by Sri. K. K. Madbavan Nair. All the 36 helper 
mistries accepted the offer of appointment and repor'ed for 
duty. It is true that the workers were called fo r interview 
for the post of mistry in the pay scale of Rs. 110-155 as is evi¬ 
dent from Ext. Wl. But Me offer of appo'ntment was to the 
post of helper mistry m the piy scale of Rs. 75-95 which 
was accepted by the 36 persons and reported for duty ard 
continued there without raisirg any objec'ion. Since there 
was an offer and acceptance and also that there was no 
legal defect in the order of appointment, it is not upon to 
the workmen to contend that Me Director had no rower 
to make such appointment. If the Director had no power to 
appoint helper mistries as contended by the union, the result 
would be that the workers were no* validly appointed to any 
post. The D : rec?or ; u exercise of the power delegated to him 
for appointment of Class FI and Class IV post tippointed 
he’per mistries and watched their performance and then 
appointed them as mjstnes. Ii was specifically s*ated in the 
order of appointment that hTher start would be riven and 
after one vear all 36 workmen were given the pay scale of 
Rs. 110-155. It may be recalled that on behalf of 34 mistries 
their association had adjudicated the same ma‘fer before the 
Industrial Tribunal Madras in I.D 3|74 and ID. 27|74 and 
that Tribunal rejected the claim of the workers. That find¬ 
ing was confirmed bv the High Court of Kerala in OP-4228! 
75 and writ appeal No. 83 77. 

5. The appo’ntment of helper mistries is an appointment 
of Class IV post in the scale of pay below Rs. 300 E.M. 
and these are posts in a lower cadre than the irbstries in 
Class III posh Both .hese cadres are in the establishment. 
Therefore there cannot be any violation o # delegation of 
financial powers. The post of he'pers is in existence and 
therefore the appointment of he’per mistries does not amount 
to creation of a post. The nersons were "elected as hel¬ 
pers for appointing them in the post of mistries in future 
and they were so appointed aFo But it does not amount to 
redesign otjon or de-grading of ‘he post. There is no altera¬ 
tion in the condition of recruitment or service. There was 
no lowering of the, quaffiention also. So there 's no viol-cion 
of O.M. No F28(l) EF 1 '88 dated 12-7-1958. These persons 
were not fixed In a lower scale but offered the helpers post 




3190 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JULY 25, 1992/SRAVANA 3 , 1914 [Part II— Sec. 3(501 


with undertaking to give higher stait watching their perfor¬ 
mance. Subsequently they were gven higher start and (he 
post of mistries. So the question of lowering the scale of pay 
does not arise. 

6 . The terms and conditions of appointment were also not 
changed. After interview these workers were kept under senior 
mistries for one year as under study and those who were 
found satisfactory were regularised as spoken to by MW2. 
MW2 has further deposed that these woikers were doing 
operations of machines and by putting them as helper mis¬ 
tries it was intended to enable them to attain the capacity 
to handle the machines indopendetly. After watching their 
performance for one year they were regularised as mistries. 
This arrangement was worked out exercising the admin¬ 
istrative discretion available to the appointing authority under 
the existing rules. Therefore the question ot violating Em¬ 
ployment Exchange Act, 1059 or any other rules does not 
also arise. Now the contention that in Exts. W3 and W4 
these persons are described a* mistries and therefore that 
proves that these persons are working as mistries from the 
date of appointment. But 'bat was clarified by MW2 who 
issued Exts. W3 and W4 i hat it was a mistake and the pur¬ 
pose of their appointment was to learn job of mistries. 
Therefore Exts. W3 and W4 does not prove that these 
workers were working as m’stries from the date of appoint¬ 
ment. 

7. From the above discussion it is clear tha 1 there is no 
merit in the contentions advanced by the union under issue 
No. I There is no illegality, injustice or violation of the 
principles of natural justice in the process of selection and 
appointment of these workmen as mistries. These workmen 
were appointed by their written consent only. Hav ng ac¬ 
cepted the offer of appointment without any objection what- 
so-ever and joined duty and continued there, these workers 
cannot now turn round and contend that process of selec¬ 
tion was illegal and 'he management has violated rules and 
circulars. There are no justifiable reasons to bold that the 
Director Production Centre has violated any rules or direc¬ 
tions of the Government of India. The result is that the 
worke-s are not entitled to any relief as claimed under 
issue No. I. 

8. The next claim of the union is regarding uniform and 
washing allowance which is included under issue No. III. 
According to the union the persons working n industrial 
establishment are provided with two sets of uniform in an 
year and are granted v/ith washing allowance. Since the 
workers in this case are appointed in Class IV category, they 
are entitled to get two sets of uniform and washing allow¬ 
ance according to the union as per Annexure-lII to Gov¬ 
ernment of India, Ministry of Hone Affairs, OM No. 5|4|58- 
Public-II dated 27-2-1962. It is opposed by the management 
mainly on the ground that these workmen arc not eligible 
for uniform as per “Hand Boole of Uniform of Gr. C and 
D Employees” since issue of un'foims to Government of 
India employees is regulated by this book issued by Gov¬ 
ernment of India. According 'o the management skilled 
workers and helpers are eligible for Aprons as per Ext. M3 
letter dated 6-6-1985. The clajm of uniform has already been 
considered and rejected by the Industrial Tribunal. Madras 
in I.D. 3|74 and 27|74 as s evident from Ext. M10 award. 
The learned counsel for the union argued that these wotkers 
are entitled to get uniform as per Annexure III to Govern¬ 
ment of India circular da'ed 27-2-1972 mentioned above. 
But it is evident from pages 52 arid 53 of Hand Book of 
uniforms mentioned above the workers in question are not 
included in the category cf employees who are elig'ble for 
uniforms in Class III and Class IV employees. Further, as 
per Ext. M3 order they are eligible to get Aprons. No evi¬ 
dence is forthcoming in support of the claim of urifoim. 
WW1 has stated that the workers are eligible for uniform 
and that they are entitled to get un'form allowance. It is 
also stated that workers handling machines and chemicals 
are eligible for uniforms as per rules. But no such rules as 
stated in the claim statemen* were brought to the notice of 
this Tribunal. There is also no evidence to show that similar 
category of workers in other establ shments are getting uni¬ 
forms. WW1 has not spoken a word about any establish¬ 
ment supplying uniform to similarly placed employees. 
MW2 has ca'CEorically denied the claim of the workmen 
which js suoported by the Hand Book of uniforms and Ext. 
M3 order. Since the anion failed to establish the elrg'biiity 
of the workers for uniform and washing allowance under 


any statute or rules as against the. documentary e'idence of 
management mentioned above, the present claim is only to 
be rejected. I do so. 

9. In view of the above discussion, I hold that the claim 
of the workers under issue No. I and the clam of uniform 
and washing allowance under issue No. Ill are devoid of 
merit and unsustainable. The workers are therefore not en¬ 
titled to any relief. 

An award is passed accordingly. 

C. N. SASIDHARAN, Industrial Tribunal

Dated, the 18th June, 1992 
AW r ARD 

By Order No. L-I2012/182/90-1.R. (B-2), dated, the 11th 
December, 1990, the Central Government in the Ministry of 
Labour, has, in exercise of the powers conferred by clause 
(d) of sub-section (1) of section 10 of the Industrial Dis¬ 
putes Act, 1947, referred the following dispute for adjudi¬ 
cation to this Tribunal : 

"Whether the claim of Bank of India Employees Cong¬ 
ress (Bihar State) that Shri Suresh Chandra tfYasad, 
Cash-Cum-Accounts Clerk in Ajwan Branch per¬ 
formed duties of Agricultural Assistant from April 
84 to April 89 and is therefore entitled to special 
allowance attached to the post of Agricultural Assis¬ 
tant Is justified 7 If so to what relief the workman 
ts entitled ?” 

2. The case of the concerned workman, as disclosed In 
the written statement submitted on his behalf by the spon¬ 
soring union, Bank of India Employees Congress (Bihar 
State!, details apart, is as follows : 

Suresh Chandra prasad, Cash-cum-Accounts Clerk was 
employed at Ajwan Branch of the Bank of India perma¬ 
nently in the year 1984. The post of Agricultural Assistant 
was created at Ajwan Branch in 1984. Shri Prascd had offi¬ 
ciated against the permanent post of Agricultural Assistant 
from April, 1984 till 1989 when a permanent-incumbent 
joined there. Clause 9.11(a) of the 1st Bipartite Settlement 
dated 19-10-66 reads as follows : 

"If a workman other than subordinate staff officiates 
in a post in a high cadre for a period of 10 days 
of more, he shall be paid an officiating allowance 
for the period for which he officiates.” 

Shri Prasad porformed to duties of Agricultural Assistant in 
nddition to his routine duties of Cash-cum-Accounts Clerk 
which is to assist in the bank’s lending and/or operations 
for agricultural development and[or financial assistance to 
small enterprises. To distribute and collect application 
fdrms and assist the farmers/small borrowers in filling up 
forms etc. The request for payment of officiating allowance 
was delayed on (he pretext of one or another till 1989. 
Anyway, the Bank had paid for a period of 4 days instead 
of a period of 5 years. The Bank has paid Agricultural al¬ 
lowance for the entire period to BrjjraJ Singh of Patna 
Branch in similar situation due to union affiliation. It Is ne¬ 
cessary to examine Whether Agriculture Department of Aj¬ 
wan Brunch has functioned only for' a- period df 4 days 
to meet the needs of the farmers since April, 1984 to April, 
1989. The concerned workman has been victimised and 
discriminated against and in the circumstances, the union 
urged upon this Tribunal to deliver judgment. 

3. The management of the Bank has contested the demand 
of the union. 

The case of the management, as appearing in the written 
statement submitted, is that Suresh Chandra Prasad was 
Cash-cum-Accounts Clerk, but he was not an Agricultural 
Assistant. He was not having the background of Agricultural 
Science to assign him duties of Agricultural Assistant. Clause 
9.10 and 9.11 of the Bipartite Settlement dated 19-10-66 pro¬ 
vides as follows : 

"Clause 9.10 : Whenever a Bank require a workman to 
officiate in post in a higher cadre, it will do so by 
an order in writing. 

Clause 9.11 ; If a workman other than subordinate staff 
officiate in a post in a higher cadre for a period 


Suresh Chandra Prasad joined the services of the Bank on 
9-4-1984. He was neither assigned nor performed the duties 
of Agricltural Assistant at Ajwan Branch. He was doing the 
following nature of work ; 

"Opening of accounts, closure of accounts documenta¬ 
tion—filling of documents, preparation of proposal, 
subsidy claim, calculation of interest etc.” 

These works are of general nature which are performed by 
a clerk of the Bank. Tire duties of Agricultural Assistants are 
enumerated in Schedule 111, Part I, Para XXI of Fourth Bi¬ 
partite Settlement dated 17-9-84. Shri Prasad never per¬ 
formed the duties of Agricultural Assistant in addition to 
his normal duties. As per the request of the Conciliation 
Officer, the Bank deputed Deputy Regional Manager and 
Deputy Chief Officer (Personnel) to investigate in the matter. 
They submitted their report on 2-4-90. The Investigating 
Officers gave sufficient opportunity to Shri Prasad to prove 
that he worked as Agricultural Assistant but failed to do so, 
Since Shri Prasad carried out post-sanction inspection for 
four days, he was paid the agricultural assistance allowance 
for these days. The Bank could not locate any record to indi¬ 
cate that Agricultural Assistance allowance was paid to 
Brijraj Singh of Patna Branch. In the context of facts and 
circumstances the management of the Bank has denied and 
disputed the claim of the sponsoring union. 

4. Neither the sponsoring union nor the concerned wl.-tI, 
man appeared at the time of bearing of the reference case. 

The management of 'he Rank examined MW-1 Sanjoy 
Kumar Mukherjee at present holding the post of Regional 
Manager, Gaya, and laid ,n evidence three items of docu¬ 
ments which have been marked Exis. M-l to M-3, 

5, The pleadings of the parties indicate that Suresh 

Chandra Prasad joined the services of the Bank at Ajwan 
Branch on 9-4-1984 as Cash-cum-Accounts Clerk. It has 
been asserted by the sponsoring union that the post of Agri¬ 
cultural Assistant was created in that branch in 1984 The 
management has not disputed this fact. The union has claimed 
that Shri Prasad had officiated against the permanent post 
of Agricultural Assistant from April, 1984 till 1989 when a 
permanent incumbent joined there. This statement of fact 
has been hotly disputed bv the managernnet. The manarement 
has asserted that Shri Prasad was never asked to officiate as 
Agricultural A-Sisfint and he was not having the backgomd 
of Agricultural Science to assign him the duties of Agricul¬ 
tural Assfstam. Clause 9 10 of the First Bipartite Settlement 
dated 19-10-66 <Fxl. M-2) provides that whenever Bunk 
requires a workman to officiate in post in a higher cadre, 
it will do so by an order in writing, Undeniably, the post of 
Agricultural Assistant is a rost in higher cadie than the 
cadre of Cashier-cum-Ao'ounts Clerk. The sponso ng union 
couM not prodti'e anv d-cum-mt to show that the concerned 
workman was directed hy Ibc management to officiate as 
Agricultural Asrvtant in writing. 

Schedule 111. P--r* 1 Para XXI of Fourth .Bipartite Settle¬ 
ment dated 17-9-1984 underlines the duties of Agricu-ltur.l 
Assistant which aic gleamed hereinbelow : (Fxt. M-l): 

"Duties of Agricultural Assistants 

(1) To assist in the bank’s lending and|or operations foe 
agricultural develoment andlor financial assitance 
to small enterprises (other than small scale 
Industrie 1 ) such as vepctable/fruit venders, artisans, 
self-emoloved rercons including beneficiaries under 
the Differential Rate of Interest Scheme. 

(2) To distribute and collect application forms and assist 
the farmers|<mall borrowers in filling up the forms. 

(3) To scrutinise anelication forms, title deeds farm 
plans etc. to ensure that applications are complete 
in all respects and the particulars furnished in the 
forms are prima facie in order and for this purpose 
he may be required t 0 visit the farms for verification 
and for collection of relevant data. 



[w'T II—*hr 3 (il)] 


3103 


BlTd 3d TriPI* : 25, 1 9 92/>«WJl 3, 191-i 


(4) To take accessary steps to ensure that the periodical 
details at called for, are received from the farmers! 
small barrowefs in time. 

(5) To keep in constant touch with fanners and to bring 
any adverse' features to the management’s notice. 

(6) To verify fann]farm machine|equipments at stipu¬ 
lated intervals in cases of loans related to agricultural 
finance upto Rs. 5,000 or cost of the pump set live 
stock or cost of agricultural implements whichever 
is higher. 

(7) To verify proper utilisation of the bank's loans or the 
progress is work in respect of which loans are 
granted and to furnish reports on such verifications. 

(8) To make efforts and effect recoveries upto amounts 
not exceeding Rs, 5,000 from fanners|small borro¬ 
wers from their place of work| residence, subject to 

the necessary arranagements being made with regard 
to fidelity and transit insurance and personal risk 
insurance by the bank. 

Note : Banks will evolve appropriate procedure about 
iviuance of provisional receipts etc, of the cash not 
exceeding Rs, 5,000 collected on a day by an agri¬ 
cultural assistant from the agricultural|small borro- 
wers[depositors. 

(9) ,To assist in the deposit mobilisation effots by en- 
^ corn-aging farmers | small borrowers to deposit their 

savings in the' bank. 

(10) To collect information about the conditions of crops 
in village. 

(11) To collect necessary data for determination of village, 
adoption for financing; of agriculture, like farmers’ 
land holdings, availability of infrastructure facilities, 
source of irrigation, use of tractors pump sets etc., 
credit facilities, available in village, recovery perfor¬ 
mance and the like. 

(12) To maintain liaison with Land Record and Registra¬ 
tion Offices and other Governmental (Developmental 
agencies for expeditious handling of the bank’s 
work. 

Note : With a view to making optimum use of tire tech¬ 
nical skill of the agricultural|de\elopment assistant, 
normally he may not be entrusted with the usual 
clerical work, but, where necessary, he may be 
asked to do the clerical work. 

The sponsoring union ha* not laid a whit of evidence to 
indicate that the concerned workman worked as Agricultural 
Assistant at the direction of the management. On the other 
hand, MW*1 Sanjoy Kumar MukherjeC presently posted as 
Regional Manager, Gaya Region of Bank of India, lias em¬ 
phatically denied that the concerned workman was posted o* 
Agricultural Assistant against permanent post from April 
1984 till 1989. He has further stated that since the concerned 
workman did not work as Agricultural Assistant at any time, 
he is not entitled to officiating allowance. He has also stated 
that at the instance of the Conciliation Officer the Bank 
investigated the matter as to whether the concerned workman 
worked at any time aj Agricultural Assistant and that he wwi 
associated with the enquiry. He has also proved the enquiry 
report which has been marked Ext. M-3. His evidence dis¬ 
closed that in the process of enquiry they called upon the 
concerned workman to produce official/relevant record or docu¬ 
ment in support of the contention that he worked as Agricul¬ 
tural Assistant and that he could produce paper in support of 
his having done as only four days for which he was paid 
special allowance. The enquiry made by the Bank in this 
matter has been marked Fxt. M-3. The report reveals that 
the Bank officials consulted the P|L Analvsis book date-wise 
for the relevant period in presence of S, C. Prasad, the ert- 
cerned workman snd found that except for four days, i.e . 
on 20-3-87. 15-4-87 and 20-4-87. Shri Prasad bad 

not gone for anv field visit for acrlcultural work and for 
these four davr he claimed travelling eypenscs on 13 6 87 
and for there four days he was already paid agricultural 
assistant’s allowance. 


6 . Upon consideration of evidence on record, I came to 
the conclusion that Surcsh Chandra Prasad, Cash-Cum- 
AccounM Clerk in Ajwan Branch did not perform official 
duties as Agricultural Assistant from April, ’84 to April ’89, 
and therefore, his claim for special allowance attached to the 
post of Agricultural Assistant during the aforesaid period is 
not justified. 

7. Accordingly, the following award is rendered the claim 
of Bank of India Employees Congress (Bihar State) that 
Shri Suresh Chandra Prasad Cash-Cura-Accounts Clerk in 
Ajwan Branch performed the duties of Agkultural Assistant 
from April ’84 to April, ’89 and is, therefore, entitled to 
special allowance attached to the post of Agricultural Assis¬ 
tant is not justified. 

In the circumstances of the case, I award no cost. 

S. K. MITRA, presiding Officer

AWARD 

The present reference arises oct of Order No. L- 
20012(260)(89-I.R.(Coal-I), dated 6-6-1990 passed by the 
Central Government in respect of an industrial dispute bet¬ 
ween the parties mentioned above. The subject matter of the 
dispute has been speified in the schedule to the said ordei 
and the said schedule runs as follows - 

■‘Whether the age assessed as 52 years as on 5-8-86 by 
the Medical Board in respect of Shri Rajdeo 
Singh, Ambulance Driver of Loyabad Centra] Hos¬ 
pital under Sijua Area No. V of M/s. Bharat Coking 
Coal Ltd. was correct? If not, what relief the work¬ 
man is entitled to T 

2. The dispute has been settled out of Court. A memo- 
r.ir, a,m of settlement has been filed in Court, I have gone 
through the terms of settlement and I ’find them quite fair 
and reasonable. There is no reason why an award should not 
be passed on the basis of terms and conditions laid down in 
the memorandum of settlement. I accept it and pass an award 
accordingly. The memorandum of settlement shall form 
part of the award. 

3. Let a copy of this award be sent to the Ministry as 
reculred under section 15 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947. 

S. K. MURA. Presiding Officer

workman is entitled to 7‘

terms and condition :—

AWARD 

By Order No. L-20012(3)i«v-i.R- (Coal-IJ, dated, the 
21st August, 1989, the Central Government in the Ministry 
of naboui, lias, in exercise of the powers eonfeircd by clause 
(uj ot sun-section (.1) and sub-section (2-A) of s-ct.on 10 of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, referred the following 
dispute tor adjudication to this Tribunal : 

‘Whether the ’action of the management of Jitpur 
Colliery of M|s. Indian Iron & Steel Co. Ltd., P.O. 
Iitpur, Dist. Dhanbad in dismissing Shri Nageswar 
Prasad Sinha, P. No. 04306 vide letter No. NJ| 
Term-Scr|87|283 dated 20-6-87 is justified 1 If not, 
to what relief the concerned workman is entitled ?” 

2. The case of the management of Jitpur Colliery of 
M|s. Indian Iron & Steel Co. Ltd., Dhanbad, as disclosed in 
the written statement details apart, is as follows : 

Nageshwar Prasad Siuha, the concerned workman, was 
employed previously as a Time Section Clerk at JiLpur Col¬ 
liery. Information relating to certain acts of misconduct 
allegedly committed by him relating to the period from 
April, 1983 to May, 1984 reached the management. Upon 
consideration of the matter, the concerned workmen was 
issued with a charge-sheet dated 11-2-1986. He submitted 
his interim reply dated 7-3-86 denying the charges and 
requested for supply of copies of documents referred to in 
the charge-sheet issued to him. The management informed 
him by its letter dated 3-4-86 that the documents referred 
to in the charge-sheet would be made available during the 
enquiry. By the same letter the management ordered a 
domestic enquiry ’and appointed Shri Mohit Mukherjee, 
Manager (Personnel), M|s. HSCO collieries as Enquiry 
Officer to conduct an enquiry into the charges levelled against 
him. The Enquiry Officer held the enquiry after due notice 
to the concerned workman who participated in the enquiry, 
All thh documents relied on by the management were pro¬ 
duced in the enquiry and the same were shown to him and 
he put his signature thereon. The management examined 
Shri R. Mohan as first witness and the concerned workman 
cross examined him partly with the assistance of his co-worker 
Bet thereafter he did not turn up for completion of cross-exa- 
m'nation of R. Mohan in spile of repeated chances given to 
him. Smeeth did not turn up in the enquiry, the next v\ itness of 
the management, K. Prasad was examined ex-parte in his 
absence. Thereafter the Enquiry Officer submitted his report 
of enuqiry dated 25-4-87. In the report the Enquiry Officer 
gave his findings to the effect that on the basts of evidence 
produced by the management in the enquiry the charges 
framed against the concerned workman were fully establ'shcd. 
Thereafter tbb matter was considered by the Manager, Nooni- 
dih-Jitpur Colliery who accepted the findings of the Enquiry 
Officer and came to the conclusion that this was a fit case 
for dismissal of the concerned workman from service. 
Thereafter the matter was also considered by the Superinten¬ 
dent (J), Noonidih-Jitpur Colliery; he also agreed with the 
views of the Manager, Noonidih-Jitpur Colliety. The case 
file was subsequently put up before the Chief Mining 
Engineer and he also accepted the findings of the Enquiry 
Officer and passed an order to the effect that the concerned 
workman should be dismissed from service. Accordingly, he 
was dismissed from service by the management vide letter 
dated 20-6-87 which was signed by the Manager. Noonidih- 
Jitpur Colliery. In the circumstances, the management has 
prayed that the action taken by the management in dismiss¬ 
ing the corcemed workman is fully justified. 

3. The case of the concerned workman, as appearing 
from the written statement submitted on his behalf by Janta 
Mardoor Sangh, briefly stated, is as follows ; 

Nageshwar Prasad Sinha. the concerned workman, was 
a permanent workman of Jitpur Colliery of M|s. IISCO. 
He was charge-sheeted by the management by memorandum 
dated 11-2-86 alongwith four annexures, namely, Articles 
of charge (Annexure-I), Statement of imputation of mrenn. 
duct or misbehaviour in support of the Articles of charge 
(Annexure-II), a list of documents numbering 33 under 17 
heads (Annexure-TJt) and a list of witnesses numbering 14 
(A nnexurc-IV). The workman concerned, submitted an 
interim reply to the charge-sheet denying the charges and 
prayed for copies of documents listed in the annexttre-III 
for exam'nntion erf the same for submitting a comprehensive 
reply. The management did not, however, consider it neces¬ 
sary to supply the copies of the documents for a comprehen¬ 


sive reply to the charge-sheet and found that the interim 
reply was unsatisfactory. A domestic enquiry was held com T 
mes,cmg from 2-8-86 during which the pnnc.ples of natural 
justice were grossly violated. The then Acting Manager, 
upon relying on the preverse and prejudicial findings of the 
Enquiry Officer, awarded the concerned workmen the 
punishment of dismissal from service. By denying to fur¬ 
nish the workman with copies of documents and withholding 
the same, the management virtually deprived him of an 
opportunity to prepare his defence. It has been alleged 
that the disciplinary action has been taken by the manage¬ 
ment vindictively as the workman had preferred an applica¬ 
tion before the Presiding Officer, the then Central Govt, 
industrial Tnhunal-Cum-Labour Court (No. 3), Dhanbad, 
under Section 33-C(2) of the Industrial Disputes Act claim¬ 
ing subsistence allowance for the period of suspension. 
There was no positive evidence against him. even then the 
management framed the charge against him on the basis of 
surmises and conjectures. At the preliminary stage the 
workman was kept unaware of any case of excess payment 
on account of overtime wages and the management with a 
malafide motive has placed the whole burden on him in order 
to nrake him a scajie-goat. The allegation of manipulation 
on the mechanised attendance sheet was not proved satis¬ 
factorily during domestic enquiry. Although 14 witnesses 
were listed to be examined by the management, only two 
witnesses were examined and the rest were withdrawn by 
the management in order to bo on the Bafe side. The evi¬ 
dence of R. Mohan, Manager (Personnel) discloses the sys¬ 
tem of work at the time office and with regard to charge 
of actual manipulation, his evidence was vague and indirect. 
The evidence of R. Prasad, Head Timekeejjer, is motivated 
in order to save his own skin. No motive of personal gain 
was alleged against the workman in the articles of charge. 
Anyway, the workman has been awarded the punishment of 
dismissal on indirect, vague and meagre evidence and there¬ 
fore, the action of the management in dismissing him from, 
service with effect from 20-6-87 is not justified. In the 
circumstances, the union has asserted that the action of 
the management in dismissing the concerned workman from 
service is unjustified and has prayed that the management 
be directed to reinstate him in service with full back wages. 

4. In rejoinder to the written statement of the sponsoring 
union, the management has stood its ground and asserted 
that the concerned workman was guilty of the charge 
levelled against him. It has been denied that the findings 
of the Enquiry Officer are perverse or that they can be 
assailed on a nr/ ground whatsoever. It is for the manage¬ 
ment to decide to examine all the witnesses or such witness 
as may be considered necessary to bring the chatge home to 
the concerned workman. It is not necessary that all the 
witnesses listed should be examined. In other words, the 
rejoinder of the management is a total rebuttal of statements 
of facts and allegation as levelled in the written statement 
of the sponsoring union. 

5. In rejoinder to the written statement of the manage¬ 
ment the sponsoring union has reiterated its own case. 

6, At the instance of the management the fairness and 
propriety ot the domestic enquiry was considered as prelimi¬ 
nary issue. In the course of hearing the preliminary issue, 
the management adduced in evidence a sheet of documents 
■which were marked exhibits. No oral evidence, however, 
was not adduced by the management. 

On the other hand, the sponsoring union examined the 
concerned workman, Nageswar Prasad Sinha and laid in 
evidence only one item of document which was marked 
Ext. W-l. 

At the conclusion of hearing on preliminary issue Shri 
D. Mukherjee, learned Advocate for the concerned workman, 
that the domestic enquiry was held fairly and properly. 
Accordingly, it was held that the domestic enquiry was held 
fairly and properly. Thereafter the case was heard on merits. 

7, Nageshwar Prasad Sinha. the concerned workman, has 
disclosed in his evidence before this Tribunal that he was 
apjvointed in Jitpur Colliery of Mis. Indian Iron A Steel 
Co. Ltd. in 1965 as Sanitary Attendant in the hospital. This 
statement of fact has not been disputed by the management. 
It appears that on 11-2-86 when the chargeshcet (Ext. M-l) 
wa 9 issued to the concerned workman he was designated 
as Qanteen Incharge but working as Celtic in Personnel 



3196 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JULY 


Department of Noonodih-Jitpur Colliery. He has slated 
that he was dismissed from service in 1987 while he was work¬ 
ing in Time Section as Clerk but his designation was 
Canteen Inchargc. This statement of his has remained 
tmassailed in cross-examination. The letter of dismissal 
dated 20-6-87 (Ext. M-39) 'also disclosed that he was working 
a clerk in Time Section of Jitpur Colliery when he was 
dismissed from service. 

8. Thus, from the evidence as discussed above it is 
evident that the concerned workman had worked in NoonJdih- 
Jitpur colliery in different capacities on different occasions. 
He was initially appointed as Sanitary Attendant in Jitpur 
Colliery in 1965. Later he was designated as Canteen 
Incharge. At the material time when he was allegedly com¬ 
mitted acts of misconduct he was posted is a clerk hi 
Time Section of Noonodih-Jitpur colliery and later was 
posted ai Clerk in Personnel Department of Noonkflh-- 
Jitpur Colliery when the chargesheet was issued. Afterwards, 
he was again brought back to Time Section as Clerk. Never¬ 
theless, the fact remains that his substantive appointment 
was as Canteen Inchargo but he was deployed for duty as 
Clerk in different Department!Section. 

9. The nomenclature and iob description of workmen in 
Coal Industry issued by J.B.C.C.I. does not envisage any 
job- description of clerical staff. Since the corcerned work¬ 
man, although designated as Canteen Incharge, was deployed 
in different Department|Section as Clerk, the management 
should have specified his duty roster in specific term and 
that too in writing. In the present case the management 
has not produced any specified duty roster in writing of 
the concerned workman. The management, has telied on 
oral evidence to prove the job description of the concerned 
workman. I will consider this aspect of the case in its 
proper place. 

10. According to the management, the concerned work¬ 
man allegedly committed the misconduct of fraud and dis¬ 
honesty in connection with the company’s business and 
property during the period from April, 1983 to March and 
May, 1984. But the chargesheet was issued to the con¬ 
cerned workman as late as on 11-2-86. 

According to Shri R. Mohan, one of the principal witness 
for the management in domestic enquiry, a High Power 
Committee, obviously set up by the management, established 
irregularities and fraud and the matter was referred to 
Internal Audit at Burnpur and Vigifance Department. In the 
context of these facts and circumstances Shri D. Mukhcrjee, 
Advocate for the concerned workman, subm tted before 
me that the management is duty bound to explain in delay 
in issuing the charge-sheet to the concerned workman. It 
may be that the matter was in a fluid stage and that could 
be the reason for delay in Issuing the charge-sheet to the 
concerned workman. 


25, 1992/SRAVANA 3, 1914 [Part II— Sec. 3(ii)J 


deuce as appearing in domestic enquiry in coming to the 
conclusion that the concerned workman was guilty of the 
charge of misconduct alleged against him' and hente non- 
production of these records is rather otiose. He submitted 
that Internal Audit report was produced in domestic en¬ 
quiry. But this report simply underlines the fact that ex¬ 
cess payments were made to some of the workmen not only 
of Jitpur Colliery but also of Chasnala Colherv and Ram- 
nagar Colliery and the Committee suggested disciplinary 
action against the delinquent employees and necessary steps 
for recovery of excess amount. This report does not pin 
point the fact that the concerned workman was one of such 
delinquent workmen. Even so, the other reports although 
not relied on by the management, should have been pro¬ 
duced in order to pin point the fact that those reports alio 
laid odium of misconduct oft the shoulder of the concerned 
workman. 

12. Then again Shri D. Mukherjee has made sensorious 
criticism of the chargesheet issued against the concerned 
workman. It is necessary to underline the facts as dis¬ 
closed in the charge-sheet which consists of Memorandum, 
Articles of Charge and statement of imputation of mis¬ 
conduct list of documents and witness. These ore re¬ 
produced below (Ext M-lj: 

”The undersigned proposes to hold an inquiry against 
Shri Nageshwar Prasad Sinha presently working as a Clerk 
in the Personnel Department and as a Clerk of Timekeeping 
Section of Noonodih-Jitpur Colliery, 1ISCO, at the mate¬ 
rial time, in terms of Certified Standing Orders of Noonidih- 
Jitpur Colliery, IISCO The substance of the imputation of 
misconduct or misbehaviour in respect of which the inquiry 
is proposed to be held is set out in the enclosed statemesnt 
of the Articles of Charge (Annexure-I). A statement of 
imputation of misconduct or misbehaviour in support of the 
said Articles of Charge is enclosed (Annexure-II). A list of 
documents by which a list of witnesses by whom the Arti¬ 
cles of Charge are proposed to be sustained ate also enclosed 
(Annexure-JTI and IV respectively). 

2. Shri Nageshwar Prasad Sinha is directed to submit 
within 7 days of the receipt of this memorandum a written 
statement of his defence and also to state whether he de¬ 
sires to b< heard in person. 

3. He is informed that the inquiry will be hold only if 
the Articles of Charge as mentioned in Anncxure 1 is not 
admitted. He, should, therefore, specifically admit or deny 
the Articles of Charge. 

4. Shri Nageshwar Prasad Sinha is further informed that 
if he does not submit his written statement of defence on 
or before the date specified in para 2 above, or does not 
appear in person before the Inquiring Authority or other¬ 
wise false, the Inquiring Authority may hold the inquiry 
against him ex-parte, 


The management has produced a note-sheet raised by 
Shri R Mohan dated 5-8-84 on the subject of tampering of 
records and making excess payment thereupon by Nageshwar 
Prasad Sinha, Time Section at Jitpur colliery presumably 
In order to explain the delay. Shri Mohan in his note 
(marked part of Ext. M-29) has stated that it was the con¬ 
cerned workman who had tampered with the record and 
that immediate step should be taken to issue chargesheet 
against him This note was meant to be put up before 
the Agent. Had the management been so sure of the mis¬ 
conduct of the concerned workman there would have no 
occasion to set up High Power Committee, to refer the 
matter to the Internal Audit of BUmpur and to tlie Vigi¬ 
lance Department. There is n 0 evidence that the note was 
put up before the Agent. Regard being had to the circum¬ 
stantial evidence ! have reason to believe that this note is 
a product of an afterthought in order to lend succour to 
the case of the management. 


tl. Shri D. Mukherjee complained that the reports of the 
Hinh Power Committee, Internal Audit of Burnpur and 
Vigilance Department has not been made available by the 
management. Shri R. S. Murthy, Advocate for the man¬ 
agement, submitted that the management relied on the evi- 


5. The receipt, of this Memorandum may be acknow¬ 
ledged.”

From the evidence of these two witnesses it appears that 
mechanised attendance sheets used to be prepared on the 
basis of attendance registers and other registers and these 
mechanised attendance sheets used to be sent to D.P.C. 
at Purnpur lor bitting 01 wages. U is the case of the 
management that the concerned workman used to post 
inflated figures in respfcct tf some daily rated workmen m 
the mechanised attendance sheet and D.P.C. used to bill 
wage-sheets on the basis ot such mechanised attendance 
sheet and in the piocesg excess payments weie made to 
these workmen and the concerned workman adopted the 
subterfuge of erasing out the inflated figures in mechanised 
attendance sheets and replaced the same by actual figure. 
In order to prove this fact that tho D.P.C. billed wage 
sheet as per mechanised attendance sheet, it was necessary 
for the management to examine witness from the D.P.C. 
ill order to sustain the fact that the D.P.C, billed the 
amount as per mechanised attendance sheet. It appears 
that one end of the rope was at Noonodih-Jitpur colliery 1 
and the other and at D.P.C. at Burn [Mr. The mechani.ed 
attendance was used to be prepared at Noonodih-Jitpur 
coll ery while billing of wage as per mechanised attendance 
sheet was done by D.P.C. at Burnpur. Hence, in my view, 
a competent person from D.P.C. should have been exa¬ 
mined by the management in domestic enquiry. 

Then again, Shri Mohan claimed in tho preliminary en¬ 
quiry that he was in a position to identify the handwriting 
of Shri Sinha, the concerned workman, in the mochaniscd 
attendance sheet for the period from April, 1983 to March— 
May, 1984. But lie did nothing of the so.t in the domis- 
tic enquiry. That being so, the allegation of erasing out of 
the inflated flguros by the concerned workman has not beien 
established to the bill. 

Shri Mohan stated that the concerned workman inflated 
the figure in respect of 32 and 26 daily rawd employees 
with mnlafide intention and ulterior motive for his per onal 
gam. Tho Enquiry Officer also held that the concerned 
workmen willfully/deliberately with malafide intention for 
the purpose of personal gain booked inflated figures in the 
mechanised attendance slice' in icspect of 52 daily rated em¬ 
ployees of Engineering Department during tho year 1983-84 
and by this fraud the concerned workman caused excess 
piiym nt to the tit'e of Rs. 1.49.905 07. Thee is not 
a whit of evidence that the concerned workman, by his 
alleged act of inflating flguro in mechanised attendance 
shoe* h 's made a"y personnel gain or that he had an ul er- 
ior motive in doing so. On the contrary, some of the work¬ 
men concerned who tecewed exerts paymc’l w\c ex- mmed 
in *be nrelim' r >j>rv enquiry a'd t.luy did not inculcate the 
Concerned workman. The management has subsequently re¬ 
alised the excess amount paid to these workmen. This being 

so, I find no objective has ; s to hold that the concerned 

workman allegedly committed any act of misconduct by in¬ 
flating tho figures of attendance of some of the daily rated 
workmen of Noonodih-Jitpur colliery from April, 1983 to 
April, 1984 for personal gain or with an ulterior motive. 

15. But the fact cannot be ignored that the concerned 

workman recorded inflated figures of attendance in respect 
of c om» workmen in the mechanised atlenda'ce sheet for 
May, 1984 But here again the allegation of personal 
grain, motive intention or ulterior mo'ivc could not be e-ta- 
bltched Thl s beine so, in my view, the concerned workman 
has committed neglect of work in performance of his duty. 
Now the miest'o- remains F dhiru'snl of 'he conc“fued 

workman from service is justified for h : s neglect of woirk. 
T^e fact cannot he ignored e->'rn after detection of alleged 

delinquency of the concerned workman, he was once again 
1774 GI/92—12, 


biought back from other section to Time Section as clerk. 
There is no evidence to indicate that he was ueployed for 
different types of job in the Time Section. Considering these 
facts and circumstances. 1 conquer that the punishment 
should be meeted out to the concerned workman not in the 
spirit of retribution but in a spirit of achieving reformation. 
Taking ih.s view, 1 think that ends of justice will be met 
If the concerned workman is reinstated in service with 40% 
of back wages. 

16. Accordingly, the following award is rendered — 

the action of the management of Jitpur Colliery of M/s. 
Indian iron & Steel Co. Ltd. in dismissing Shri Nageshwar 
Prasad Sinha is not justified. The management is directed 
to reinstate him in service with effect from the date of his 
dismissal from service with 40% of back wages with conti¬ 
nuity of service and other benefits withn one mon'h from 
the date of publication of the award m the official Gazette. 
The concerned workman is directed to report for duty 
within the time stipulated. 

In the circumstances of the case, I award no cost. 

This is my award.

Tribunal, Them is no paper to sohw that actually any writ¬ 
ten examination was conducted. The witness staled that in the

S.o. 1988 —In pursuance of Section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government 
hereby publishes the Award of the Central Government In¬ 
dustrial Tribunal (No. 2), Oh ah bad as shown in the An- 
nexure in the Industrial Dispute between the employers in 
relation to the management of Saunda-D Colliery of M|s. 
C.C. Ltd. and fficir workmen, which was received hv the 
Central Government on 29 6-92. 

[No. L-240l2(24)/88 D.IV B/Vol-ll]

ANNEXURE 

BEFORE THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRiAT. 
TRIBUNAL (NO. 2) AT DHANBAD 

PRESENT : 

Shri B. Ram, Pres'ding Officet 

In the matter of an industrial dispute under Section 
10(1) (d) of the I.D. Act, 1947. 

REFERENCE NO. 33 OF 1988 

PARTIES : 

Employers in relation to the management of Saunda-D 
Colliery of M|s. C.C. Lid, and their workmen

Dhanbad, the June, 1992 

AWARD 

The Govt, of India, M nistry of Labour, in exercise of the 
powers conferred on them undet Section 10(1)(d) of the 

I.D. Act, 1947 has referred the following dispute to this 
Tribunal for adjudication vide their Order No. L-24012|24l84- 
D. IV(B)|Vol. IL dated the 20th lanuaiy. 1988. 

SCHEDUrE 

"Whether the action of the Management of Saunda-D 
Colliery of M|s. Central Coalfields Limited, P O. 
Saunda, Distf. llazaribugh in stopping Sri Banditti 
Mahato, Ex-Haulage Khalasi from wotk, is justi¬ 
fied 7 Jf not, to what relief the workman concerned 
is entitled 7" 

2. Shri Bandhu Mahato, the concened workman has claimed 
reinstatement with lull back wages and other benefits by 
M/s. C.C. Ltd. with effect from 1-5-73. As per the state¬ 
ment of his claim he was Haulage Khalasi at Sunda ‘D’ 
coll'tory of M[s. Karanpura Colliery Ltd. with effect from 
1958. He stated that he was falsely implicated by the mana¬ 
gement in a criminal case which w'as instituted on the basis 
of the report submitted by the Medical Officer Shri A.D. 
Singh of Saunda ‘D’ colliery with Bhurkundu Police outpost. 


It is further Mated that fhcie was a strike at the coll'ery and 
after it was withdrawn the concerned w orkman and other 
teported for duty hut they were stopped and it was stated 
on behalf of the management that their set vice have already 
been terminated as they were involved in Criminal Case 
hearing G. R. Case No 1387 68 under Section 147, 4*2, 
323. 125, 379 of I. P, C 

3. The colliery in due com sc was nationalised and after 
nationalisation the concerned workman again demanded 
from the management to allow him to resume his duties as 
no formal Older stopping him from service was passed. 
The concerned workman also stated that the erstwhile 
management had assured him that his case for taking him 
hack on job would be considered only after the disposal of 
the criminal case. Be that as it may, the matter rema ned 
hanging till the disposal of the criminal case and ullimately 
a judgement was passed in the Criminal Case on 5-5-80 
wherehy the concerned W'orkman was found not guilty anil 
acquitted. Again the concerned workman approached the 
management but it was of no use and then he rased in¬ 
dustrial dispute before the RLC(C), Dhanbad. The matter 
could not be settled in the conciliation ptoceeding and the 
failure report was submitted to the Government. Unfor¬ 
tunately the Government of India decided not to refer the 
industrial dispute to Tribunal for adjud cation because the 
name of the concerned workman was not found in the 
register of the collierv when the control and management 
of the colliery was taken over by the Govt, Ultimately the 
concerned workman had to knock the door of the 
Hon’b]e High Court and the matter was decided through 
Writ Petition No. CWJC. 1251 of 1984(R' 1 The Hon’ble 
Court directed the Government to make reference and 
hence the present reference, 

4. The management has filed W.S. denying the clam of 
the concerned workman, Firstly it was staled that the claim 
was overstale and hence not maintainable. The next legal 
point urged w'as that the Parliament enacted the Coal 
Mines Laws (Amendment Act), 1986 whereby Section 14 
of the Qoal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1973 was substi¬ 
tuted by some other provisons with effect from 7-10-86. 
It was contended that Section 14(1) of the Coal Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act was completely deleted by the afote- 
said amendment act of 1986. It wus urged that in view of 
the amendment the concerned workman has got no right to 
claim any employment under the management. 

5. Shri Bandhu Mahato W'as working as Pump Khajasi in 
Saunda ‘D’ Colliery of M|s. Bird A Co. (a private company) 
and on account of certain ac f s of misconduct on his part 
lie w'as chargesheeted on 27-7-68 under the Certified Stand- 
ding Orders of the Colliery. During the course of enquiry 
the management found him guilty. The enquiry report wus 
examined and Bandhu Mahato was dismissed from service 
with effect from 14-10-68. The management has to say 
that in this view of the matter the concerned workman was 
dismissed and never stopped as claimed by him. His dis¬ 
missal was by the erstwhile management and not by the 
present management of Saunda 'D' Collierv of M|s C.C. L. 
In this way it was prayed that the claim Iprayer of the union 
should be disallowed. 

6 . Now from the pleading of the part es it is evident 
that Shri Bandhu Mahato win an employee of Saunda ‘D’ 
Colliery of Bird & Co., a private company. The workmen 
claim that there was a general strike in the company in 
the year 1968 and he was fajselv implicated in a criminal 
case. After the strike was over he teported for duty but he 
was stopped but the management on the other hand allege 
1 hat the concerned workman wa» chargesheeted for the mis¬ 
conduct committed by him and after domestic enquiry he 
was dismissed from the job of the company with effect 
from 14-10-68. However, the concerned workman has de¬ 
nied this assertion of the management. On the recoid theie 
was no legal evidence to show that any domest'c enquiry 
was conducted for any act of misconduct on the part of 
(he concerned workman. Only the photo copy of the true 
conv of enquiry proceeding was avjHablb wTikih legally 
could not have been brought on the record, In the begin¬ 
ning a few' witnesses weie examined on the point to ascer¬ 
tain as to whether any dome die enquiry had been conducted 
or not for alleged misconduct but that woulc have proved 
to be simply an exercise in futility and hence it was decided 
and held that full Hedged c' idencc should be adduced to 



[«tr<r 3 (ii} ] 


»TTCT sn I 25. 19 92/'*im 3. 1914 


3203 


prove the charges against the concerned workman as if the 
domestic enquiry if any hejd in this case were found to Ic 
unfair and against the principles of natural justice, The 
parties were accordingly directed and witnesses were exa¬ 
mined, At this stage L may refer to Ext. M-3 which is a 
rejoinder submitted by the union to the ALCHC), Dhanbad 
therein it has been stated that Shn Bandbu Mahuto Khalasi 
was dismissed from service by ex-owner of Saunda ‘D’ 
colljery at the time of strike. In such view of the matter 1 
am to hold that the concerned workman was dismissed und 
not stopped from the service. 

7. The photo copies of the true copies of chargeshect 
and dismissal order have been marked ‘X’ and ‘V’ respec¬ 
tively for identification wiii ;h cannot be legally referred to. 
Admittedly there was a criminal case and certified copy of 
Lire FIR is on the recurd (Ext. W-2). The recital of the 
FIR will show that one incident took place on 17-7-68 at 
about 7-30 A.M. when a mob consisting of about 17 per¬ 
sons shouting slogan marched to the office of the Manager. 
They smashed the window panes of the office of the mana¬ 
ger and other allied officers and also caused injury to the 
staff. Conspicuously, it may bo mentioned here that the 
name of the concerned workman does not find any reference 
in the FIR. Of course one Bandhu Bedia is there hut there 
is nothing to show that Bandhu Mahato and Bandhu Bedia 
are one and the same man. Here it may be noted that re¬ 
ference of the MR has become necessary in view of the fact 
that there is no chargesheet and the allegation as alleged 
by the management was almost similar to the allegations 
lodged with the Police. It is said that Bandhu Mahato was 
chargesheeted by the Police but after full dress trial by the 
Crim'nal Court he was held not guilty and so acquitted and 
discharged from the liublity of his ba'l bonds. Certified 
copy of the judgement of the Criminal Court is Ext. W-3 
Admittedly, the judgement of the Criminal Court cannot 
have any binding effect upon the charge levelled by the 
management and the order passed thereunder. However, the 
learend counsel for the workmen contended that it has got 
it s own use and value for collateral purpose. At this stage 
one authority has been cited on behalf of the management 
reported in 1985 Vol. 11 f.LJ page 364 wherein their Lord¬ 
ship held that domestic enquiry can be proceeded even after 
acquittal by the Criminal Court. This means the judgement 
of the Criminal Court c ther of acquittal or conviction is 
no bar for the departmental enquiry. Anyway, in the instant 
case wc see that the domestic enquiry was held much earlier 
and the result of the criminal Court came after about 12 
years. 

8 . Now let us see how far the management has been able 
to prove the chaiges against the concerned workman. From 
the evidence of the witness it appears that the main charge 
against the concerned workman was that he was a member 
of the mob and he had actively participated in rioting. 
Actually the allegation in the FIR was also the identical. 
The management has examined 6 witnesses out of which 
MW-1 Shri B.D. Singh, MW-2 S.P. Tewary, MW-3 Badri 
Prasad Singh and MW-6 Shri P. K. Malkar claimed to have 
seen the occurrence. Now we will seo how far they arc 
competent to speak about the occurrence as eye witness. 
Out of these four witnesses Shri Badri Pd. Singh is the only 

witness who had also deposed before the Trial Court. Re¬ 

maining three witnesses were admittedly not examined in 
the criminal Court. This will be suggestive of the fact that 
they were not an eye witness otherwise the prosecution 
must have examined them in the criminal court. The copy 
of the chargesheet submitted by the Police is not on the re¬ 
cord to show as to whether they figured as witness or not. 

If thev were the witness in the chargesheet then the pre¬ 

sumption would be that they wilfully avoided their appear¬ 
ance in the Court as witness. Here in this Tribunal they 
claim to have b;en the occurrence. They have named the 
concerned workman ns one of the members of the unlawful 
assembly participating in the incident, MW-1 stated that 
Bandhu Mahato was raising slogan Mato Kalo. MW-3 
stated that the mob was shout ng slogan as (Sale ko maro 
pakro, jan se maro). Admittedly, the FIR before the Police 
was lodged by Shri AD. Singh, Welfare Officer of Bird 
Saunda colliery, In the FIR there is no whisper of such 
unpleasant words lo have been uttered by any member of 
the mob and the simple inference would be that such state¬ 
ment here before this Tribunal has been introduced a» au 
after thought most probably to magnily the severity of the 
.’ticident although little knowing about its adverse effect. 


MW-1 cannot say as to who were the informant before the 
Police. Naturally he did not tel] anything about the occu¬ 
rence to the informer, MW-2 also did not tell the informant 
about the occurence. The question is if they had seen any 
occurrence they were expected to have told the informant 
before an FIR was lodged with the Police. 

9. MW-3 is the witness who was also exam ned in the 
criminal court at Hazaribugh. He stated in unequivocal 
terms that he had not numed Bandhu Mahato before the 
Criminal Court. It remained quite unexplained as to what 
prompted this witness to tell the name of the concerned 
workman before this Ti bunal when according to his own 
admission he had not mentioned his name before the Cri¬ 
minal Court. 1 think the only infeience would be that he 
was tutored to state the name oi Bandjiu Mahato. 

10. MW-6 Shri P. K. Malakar was Btated to be in survey 
room at the time of occurrence and he claimed to have been 
the occurrence and identified the concerned workman while 
hiding himself behind a table for the members of the mob 
were throwing brick bats. At the time of any incident the 
hides on account of lear being assaulted and in the silu- 
at on he cannot be permitted to say that he had taken full 
view of the occurrence. Again he says that the FIR of this 
case was lodged by him but the truth is that the same was 
registered by Shri A.D. Singh, the Welfare Officer. On the 
whole the evidence of the witness does not inspire any con¬ 
fidence. MW-4 and MW-5 arc formal witness and they have 
proved a few documents which have been marked Ext. M-l 
to M-3. 

11. From the evidence as discussed above I am to hold 
that none of the so-called eye witness except MW-3 ever 
saw any part of the occurrence and so their statement before 
the Tribunal that they had seen Bandhu Muhato In the 
mob cannot be expected as true version and thus it is 
manifest that the management has completely failed to 
prove that the concerned workman was a member of any 
lawful assembly and that he actively participated in the 
occurrence. 

12. A few law points have been rased on the point of 
the reference. It has been urged that the reference has been 
made after 20 year of the occurrence and so it is overstale 
and cannot be maintained under the law. Two authorities 
as reported in SCIJ Vol. 1 page 104 and SOLI Vol. 4 
page 228 have been cited. The concerned workman was 
dismissed in the year 1963 and the reference was made 
in the vear 1988 thus giving n wide gap of about 20 years. 
But before giving a tad name to the reference we are also 
to see as to how far the concerned workman was responsi¬ 
ble for this abnormal delay. Here 1 may refer to the 
exhibits field on behalf of the management. Ext. M-l is an 
application filed by the concerned workman to the Director 
of Personnel, CCF Ranchi requesting for reinstatement. In 
the application there is a reference that the concerned work¬ 
man had also filed an application on 6-8-80, This document 
atlcast shows that from the year 1980 the concerned work¬ 
man was vigilant in persuing the matter. M-2 is the en¬ 
dorsement of Dy. C.M.E. on Ext. M-l, From Ext. M-3 it 
appears that in the year 1973 there was conciliation. We 
have no other document that the dispute was raised any 
time prior to 1980. It may be remembered that he was 
acquitted by the criminal court in the year 1980 itself. 
There is one more document on behalf of the workman 
which is Ext. W-l. This document has been branded by the 
management as forged and fabricated. This js dated 
31-1-1973 and it was a letler written by the Colliery Mana 
per to the Secretary Koynla Mazdoor Union Saunda Cojliery 
Hazaribagh stating that the case of Bandhu Mahato for his 
reinstatement will be considered after his acquittal in the 
Criminal case. Prirna fac : e I find no reason as to why this 
document should be called forged and fabricated. But the 
question again arises as to why reinstatement when he was 
already dismissed from the service. At best it can be said 
that the concerned workman did not take any step till 1980 
i.e, far about 11 years, Possibly that may be on account of 
'Imorance or misunderstanding. It seems that In the year 
1983 an industrial dispute was raised but lastly the Central 
Govt, refused to make anv reference on the ground that 
the name of Bandhu Mahito was not in the employment of 
the colliery, which was nationalised under the Co a] Mines 
(Nationalisation) Act, 19/3. However, the matter went up 



3204 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JULY 25, 


before the Hon'ble Couit and it was lcfcried to ths Tii- 
bunal. Thus fiom the vcar 1932 to 1988 theie was no 
latches on the part oi the conccined workman of course in 
the beginning theie ,vas sonic delay which may be attributed 
to the ignorance on the part of the concerned workman but 
on the whole there docs not appear to be anything inten¬ 
tional. In tho ciioumstanees, 1 am not inclined to refused 
the chum of reinstatement of the coneined workman on the 
ground of overstate retell nee It is true that ihero s no 
limitation prescribed tn icference of a dispute to an Indus¬ 
trial Tribunal even so it is icasonable that the dispute 
should be refer, cd as soon as possible after they have 
arisen and after conciliation pioceedng failed. It is also 
true that generally over dale claim should not he allowed 
or tncouraged unless theie is satisfactoiy explanation for 
delay. Whether a claim has become too stale or not will 
depend upon the facts of each case. I have already held 
that in the instant case the concerned woikman alone was 
not very much responsible for such abnormal delay m the 
reference. 

13 The next point uiged by the learned counsel for the 
management was that n view of the Coal Mines (Nationali¬ 
sation) Laws (Amendment! Act. 1986 the concerned woik¬ 
man stands no where and he cannot be leinstated. It was 
pointed out that by an act of amendment of Section 14(1) 
of the Coking Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1973 has 
been deleted. In ibis connection an umrported authority 
vide CWJC. 1747 of 1988 (R) was also lcfened to wherein 
his Lordship was pleased to hold that the amended act of 
1986 has got its retrospective effect and substituted section 
14 came into force with effect from 1-5-73. It was further 
hold that as the four workmen were not in the employment 
in the Coal Mines in question on the appointed date i e. 

1 5-73 respondent No. 2 could not have directed their 
reinstatement, 

14. Heie in the piesant icference vve find that definitely 
Bandhu Mahato was not in the employment of the company 
on 1-5-73 but the question s as to hew he could have been 
in the employment when he was already dismissed in the 
year 1968. Foicibly lie could not have remained in the em¬ 
ployment of the company, when he was already 
dismissed and removed I have already held that his 
dismissal from the service was illegal, unwananted 
and without jurisdiction and the position would be 
that he was still in the employment of the company on 
1-5-73. In such view of the matter I do not think this pio- 
vision of Coal Mines National]'iiilion Amended Act, 1986 
can be strictly applied in the case of Bandhu Mahato. But 
it is equally true that Bandhu Mahato jemained out of 
job for about ?0 years and timing this period ho did not 
do any duty of the company. There was ‘ome latches on 
the part of the concerned woikman as held above. I theje- 
fore do not propose to give him full back wages for the 
whole of the peiiod, 

In the result, I hold that the action of the management 
of Snunda-D collieiv of M|s Cential Coalfields limited, 
P.O. Saunda. District Harurbagh in stopping Sri Bandhu 
Mahato, ex-Haulage Khalasi from work is not justified. 
Consequently, the concerned woikman is cn'itled to be 
reinstated in his job with cfleet from 1-5-73 with payment 
of 40 percent back wages fiom the date of icference and 
other consequential I enefifs bv the Ms. CCI The manage¬ 
ment is therefore iirec'cd to reinstate the concerned woik¬ 
man tn his original job wcf. 1-5-73 with payment of 
40 percent bark wages fiom the daN of refcience und 
other consequential benefits within one month fiom the date 
of publication of the Award. 

This is my Award. 

lL RAM, Presiding Officer

SO. 1989.— Jn pursuance of Section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government 
heerby publishes tho award of the Central Government In¬ 
dustrial Tribunal, Kanpur as shown in the Aunexure in the 
industrial dispute between the employers in relation to the 
management of Bank of India and then workmen, which 
was received by the Central Government on the 26 6-92. 

[No. L-12012/137/87-D.II(A)J 
V. K. VENUCOPALAN, Desk Officer 
ANNEXURE 

BEFORE SRI ARIAN DEV, PRESIDING OFFICER.
